i-a3d72c2ab376980f-9	Madam President, I would like I agree with our reservation, but for time-winning, I am content to support the committee's proposal. When I thought about what I would say in this fourth debate on the activities of the penitentiary that we have during our term of office, I looked through the last four years' annual reports from the Prison Service and was struck by the fact that it is a fairly prosperous activity. That the fag company is managed in an exemplary way is the picture I get, not only from dry figures and annual reports from the agency but also by reading the JO notifications and the Parliamentary Ombudsman's annual report. I also see how you work with what you are not very good at and get criticism for, because there is of course a lot in the prison service where you do not reach the end of the mission. The knowledge that we politicians have gained when we have visited prisons, prisons and outpatient activities also verifies the image of the operation being well functioning. Drug use, which is often mentioned, is low, 1 – 2 percent in screening tests, and escapes are very rare, although we had some incidents in the autumn. Let me make two points. One is that I am very grateful for the fact that the prison service is based on a scientific basis. It is a most refreshing approach at this time of scientific denunciation and mistrust of the facts. Not least in the programme activities and treatment methods, after evaluations and validations, the methods that do not work are continuously cleaned out. This is something that other policies should pursue a little more, especially in view of some liberal school politicians and party leaders. The recidivism rate is dropping. This may be due to the methodical and scientifically based work. This can, of course, also be the result of the fact that the other law enforcement authorities have not been effective enough in recent decades. However, I would like to believe that at least part of the fact that the relapse rate has decreased by more than 10 percentage points is the result of successful systematic structured work in the area of penitentiary care. The second point I would like to make is that the prison system is based on the basic values that are basically embraced by all of us. It is about humanistic, democratic values of the staff. If we are to be completely honest, there were in the past – or in the good old days, as some parties might say – some things to do about the values of the staff. The fact that the prisoners have problems with the values is quite obvious, because that is one of the reasons why they are sitting there. But the staff, who work closely with these individuals, must stand on a solid foundation. I usually say that on my study visits to the prison services I meet more humanists than in politics. The staff is extremely animated by their work in the tough environment in which they operate as professionals. That's why I feel safe. In many political corridors, core work is almost a scolding word today, but it is a necessary tool for penitentiary staff to be able to do a good job. Correctional services stand out in many different ways, not least in international comparisons. One of the most important things is that you work so close to your clients, close to the individuals. Not only do the prisoners themselves stand for the formation of standards and structures, but they are close. Another thing is the dynamic security work. Both of these are success factors that many visitors from all corners of the world look at and try to take the best of. This, of course, costs. Compared to many other countries, we have an expensive prison service per inmate. But for society it is a particularly good deal. Correctional services are very world-oriented, which is a strange paradox given the fact that walls are being built against the outside world because the individuals inside involuntarily have not been able to cope with the surrounding world and the norms and values that are out there. The very idea of an institution is to shut out the outside world. Yet the world out there comes inside the walls and the Gunnebo fences. When the problems of extremism on the right-wing political side or religious Islamism increase, it is evident inside the walls, and then the prison services must face this with increased awareness and concrete action plans. The crime rate in society is decreasing overall. Violent crime has been declining for a long time, not least because we drink less alcohol. When we drink less, we do fewer stupid things, and that alcohol consumption has almost halved among young people, who are the most active in crime in society, of course also affects the prison services, both in terms of flows and how to work with this. Particularly in our particularly vulnerable areas, the organised criminal gangs are putting their head on how we deal with all the tools of the rule of law. This also requires completely different conditions for planning prisoners. For a long time, people have worked hard to separate gang members from each other. It was perhaps easier at the time when you were wearing vests or when the gang structures were fixed. Today it is not; today it is fleeting, and friendships are broken and changed. The Metoo discussion also comes into the prison system and among the prison staff. It's obvious. There are a lot of challenges that you face. Staff turnover is perhaps the largest. It is likely that the operation will have to be split up because the judicial system may be more effective when the police are fully organised. It may be possible to open up new institutions, or rather expand existing ones. We have given assignments from the Riksdag to the Prison Service regarding transport to relieve the police. We also need to review interfaces and ensure that capacity is expanded. We must be able to continue working with the business to break the isolation. There's still a lot to do, not least in our detention centres. Finally, I want to raise my eyes a little. Sweden is doing very well – remarkably well, you might say. We are one of the world's most well-managed countries and have been so throughout the post-war period – even during the bourgeois years, I might add. This is, of course, something that sticks in the eyes of the worst dystopians, who, moreover, are often populists. They are the ones who feast on stigmatising already vulnerable groups and who bring out the old usual trick of looking for scapegoats – the easiest trick in the political toolbox. They do not hesitate to exploit politically tragic events and events in society. Sometimes, as an old man, I feel that I wish for a somewhat more quiet craft and a structured long-term approach to the political debate. As far as the prison services are concerned, I think I'd like to say that's the case. We have not had much discussion and not much trouble in committee about it, and that is very nice. This has been the policy of the long waves. We also agree fundamentally on what we have said so many times, namely that the best behaviour not to get new clients in the prison system is to keep society together, to make sure to work crime prevention, to care for the activities that build people and that create strong societies and to prioritise welfare before tax cuts. Actually, it's that simple, and I think we can agree on that.

Longer sentences neither reduce crime nor deter people from committing crime, and there is nothing to show that a person becomes less criminal if they sit in prison for a long time; rather, they become more criminal. This is not something that I have invented, but it is national and international research that shows that it is just like this. Therefore, the Left Party proposes that no one should be in prison with a sentence of less than a year. If, on the other hand, a person is sentenced to a sentence of more than one year, the person must be in an institution, as it is the most effective method of preventing the person from committing a crime. That is why we propose that people should receive their treatment within the framework of care, and that it should be combined with intensive monitoring with electronic anklet. Will it have a greater effect on relapses in crime after intensive surveillance over a long period of time in the outpatient care? Yes, it probably will be. Punishments that are carried out in prison can be made longer, more intervention and designed more content-rich and personalised. In an institution, opportunities are limited to own resources. On the other hand, in society, the client must manage his or her own life and deal with the risk situations that have led to crime in the past. The right choice of sanctions is an important prerequisite for the best possible success of the work of the penitentiary and for the most efficient use of their resources. People who have committed crimes are part of society. They will return to society after execution. On this basis, it is very difficult to understand the point of the widespread use of short prison sentences. The Left Party therefore believes that the number of short prison sentences should be reduced and that the use of intensive monitoring with electronic control should be extended. I'm in favour of reservation 16.

i-a3d72c2ab376980f-191	Madam President, I would like Today we are debating the SoU7 report. In the report, the committee examines the Government's letter The Swedish Audit Office's report on the over-use of dental support as a result of over-treatment. The 2008 Allian Government's dental reform and the introduction of the special dental allowance and support for long-term illness or disability from 2013 resulted in a significant strengthening of the state's dental support. Already in the first year nearly SEK 6 billion was allocated to the dental reform. We want to safeguard this reform. It is today Försäkringskassan that pays compensation to dental practitioners and manages the administration of the state dental support. This management is mainly automated via an electronic system, where dental practitioners report through this electronic system and receive compensation. The system also allows automatic checks on the data reported. This electronic system means, among other things, that in the event that something does not match, the operator may not receive any compensation. There is therefore a control mechanism in the electronic system. So far, there's nothing weird about it. However, automatic checks cannot completely prevent incorrect application of the regulatory framework. There is therefore a need for manual control and sometimes subsequent follow-up. Madam President, I would like In the report we are discussing today, the Swedish National Audit Office has chosen the method of conducting a field experiment. This meant that 735 dental clinics were selected, both private clinics and public dental clinics. These clinics made up about 15 percent of the country's dental clinics. The selection was also made from the perspective of reflecting the distribution between private receptions and public dental clinics, i.e. eight public dental clinics against ten private practitioners. Nor is there any problem with the Swedish National Audit Office's structure of the audit. In addition, the Swedish National Audit Office points out that fraud and outright cheating are outside the scope of this review. It is a serious problem that the decisive method that the Swedish National Audit Office has chosen to use is based on assumptions about the dental measures 800 and 801. These measures are in this case dental crowns. In the case of action 800, a patient receives only one dental crown in a jaw. If, instead, the patient receives several dental crowns in the same jaw at the same time of treatment, only dental crown action 801 should be applied. In other words, these are two completely different methods and two different treatments. Thus, it is difficult – or in this case impossible – to equate these two measures. The Swedish National Audit Office, on the other hand, claims that the over-use of dental support can be demonstrated by the fact that, via prior information, half of the dental clinics in the field experiment have provided these clinics with information that their treatments for a particular measure would be followed up and checked afterwards. The Swedish National Audit Office believes that if this prior information leads to a change in treatment patterns in the form of a reduction in the measure under control, it is a strong sign that overtreatment occurs. However, since the selected treatments examined are not interchangeable, logic does not hold up. In addition, the criticism is directed at the fact that the Swedish National Audit Office in its review was based on a regulatory framework that was repealed already in 2008. It is good that the Government also notes the clear shortcomings in the Swedish National Audit Office's review report. At the same time, what is clear is that the government cannot fail to allow ideology to take precedence over facts and analysis in the letter. Private dental care in Sweden has the highest satisfaction among patients of all activities evaluated by the Swedish Quality Index. In addition, the Social Insurance Agency, which administers this system and also carries out regular manual ex-post checks, has concluded that, in general, no clear signals or patterns can be established indicating that the state dental aid is being overused. In 2015, the Swedish Social Insurance Agency also carried out its own checks. In a number of cases, it was established that errors which led to recovery of compensation paid had occurred. In five of the cases, the errors were considered to be so serious that the dental clinics were reported to the police. Of SEK 5.3 billion in compensation paid in 2015, it is estimated that recovery will amount to SEK 13.6 million. Although the Swedish National Audit Office states that their review does not cover fraud and outright cheating, and despite the fact that the Government's own authority The Swedish Social Insurance Agency does not believe that it is possible to draw conclusions on general patterns of over-use of state dental support, the Government writes in its letter that there are indications that the state system is being abused. It is abused, on the one hand, by healthcare providers for financial gain and, on the other hand, by operators who in some cases are deemed to be linked to organised crime. It goes without saying that all fraud and fraud involving taxes should be prosecuted and criminals prosecuted. The allocation of common resources intended for use in welfare shall always be closely, systematically and regularly monitored. From there, however, the step is far to talking sweepingly about indications and about the misuse of dental support by certain private dental providers. The Alliance parties share the government's view that the Social Insurance Agency should be given enhanced control and sanctioning possibilities. It is a good thing that the conditions are now being examined to give Försäkringskassan increased opportunities. This also applies to the possibility to unsubscribe dental practitioners who are misbehaving from the replacement system. At the same time, however, it would be even better for the government to establish the clear principle that all welfare activities, regardless of direction, should be carried out on equal terms. In addition, there is supervision, control, follow-up and evaluation of all dental practitioners regardless of their type of operation. That, Madam President, is the basis for why we have a social service. When the nine-year-old girl stands there without parents, our welfare state steps in and says that she too should have the chance to make her dreams come true. And here I am, Madam President, proud and grateful for the strong welfare state that was there for me. Of course, my personal life experience gives me no preference for interpretation but a good understanding that all people can find themselves in a situation where they need the support and help of society. Today we are debating and discussing errors and shortcomings, things that need to be improved and laws that need to be changed and renewed. That is good, because we should always have the individual vulnerable child in our eyes, and we will always need to develop and improve the strong society organisation so that we can capture every single child when needed. When, after the 2014 elections, I entered the Riksdag and was given responsibility for, among other things, social services and children's issues in the Parliamentary Social Committee, I quickly realised that social services were badly neglected. I could stand here in the pulpit claiming that it was the eight years in power of the bourgeois government that were the reason for this, but the truth is that social services have been politically removed much longer than that. As a Member of Parliament, during my many visits to social services around the country, I have met a hard-pressed and neglected social service. It was often extremely pressured social security secretaries who received me and who testified about concerns that have been raised, about investigations that have not been made and about a stressed working environment. While pressure on social services had increased over a long number of years, the shortage of staff and the high turnover of staff were also a brake on the proper functioning of social services. Of course, this led to an increasing number of clients having to compete for the skills, treatment, time and money of social services. I have also met young newly graduated social secretaries who have received little or no introduction at all and who have had to deal with the toughest cases from day one. It was the new social secretary who came into the social service full of energy but who quickly realized that the social work, what they wanted to do, was out of time and that the energy had to be spent on all the documentation that had been left behind. To say the least, Madam President, I experienced a social service that needed more of us politicians. I am not going to stand here today and beat myself to the chest and claim that we have solved all the problems in social services during this term of office, when we have been in power. On the contrary, this is an area of constant challenges. But we've done a lot. Since day one, we have been working hard on issues relating to social services. For the first time ever, there has been a direct targeted strengthening of social care for children and young people in the form of a billion-dollar investment over four years to go precisely towards human resources enhancement, upgrading skills and quality. 10 extra welfare billions have been allocated to municipalities annually from 2017. It is money that should definitely benefit social services. Social secretarial assistants are now being hired, who may be the extremely important resource in social services that enables the social secretary to engage in social work. We have ensured that young newly graduated social secretaries can take part in an important web-based support for career introductions. We have increased the national standard for support for families with children, increased child benefit by SEK 200 per child, made more money available for summer holiday activities for children and ensured that women's shelters around the country received much-needed increased support. And, Madam President, we are finally turning the Convention on the Rights of the Child into Swedish law. The new law clarifies that courts and legal practitioners must take into account the rights deriving from the Convention on the Rights of the Child. Although Sweden has undertaken to comply with the Convention on the Rights of the Child, there are shortcomings in both legislation and practice. The incorporation of the Convention on the Rights of the Child contributes to the visibility of the rights of the child. It is a way of creating a basis for a more child-right-based approach in all public activities. The Act is proposed to enter into force on 1 January 2020. Over the 30 years, our society has changed a lot. The many changes in society have meant that social services have been given responsibility for new groups, tasks and challenges. This, of course, places great demands on municipalities' social services and their staff. We want a social service that works with early and preventive efforts for children, women who are victims of violence, people with substance abuse problems and other groups and that the efforts should strengthen those who turn to social services. Preventive work, as part of the development of social work and preventing people from falling into line and becoming subject to official practice from the outset, is an important part of the social services inquiry. Having said that, Madam President, I would like to endorse Amendment No 36 and the proposal in the rest of the report.

Whether the Riksdag considers the proposals to be good or not, the routine is that they are rejected. It may sound strange, but good proposals can then be processed, for example, in the departments and come back to Parliament as proposals from the government – what is called proposals. This is probably the most common time for the proposals we Members make during the general period of exercise. This procedure is intended to ensure that proposals adopted in this House have been examined and nailed by the main lawyers; that is what we have legislative referrals for. Of course, we do not want Parliament to be hasty and to make decisions that can have unexpected and very difficult consequences. It can be said that this approach is Parliament's method of practicing what is called the precautionary principle in the EU. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-279	Mr President, I would like to Today we have gathered in this House to debate the class society for once, or at least those who pay the price for it. Social services are the ultimate safety net our welfare society has. That is where we come when our life situation is truly untenable. It is social services that should ensure that we do not fall completely outside the framework of society or end up in total poverty. But social services are also a temperature meter when it comes to how our country is doing and how widespread the class society is. The more people need to have their rights met through social services, the greater the gaps between people. How we then respond to those seeking support from social services is a direct reflection of the human outlook that prevails in our country. The understanding of the importance of class society for people to end up in social or economic vulnerability was one of the reasons for the emergence of the social services law we now have. In the past, the poverty laws had been marked by strong control of people, who were to be disciplined to repentance – a view based on the starting point that the poor was unsuspecting and had himself to blame. The new law came to take a completely new direction with an understanding of the impact of social structures on people. From the point of view of the individual, social services would now be service-oriented and helpful. It was a law that would stand up for people's value and secure their rights. Today, social services are in deep crisis. In a report after a report, we can read about how the work situation of social secretaries is becoming increasingly unsustainable. We see an increase in sick leave, an increasing number of people experiencing stress and more and more people leaving work because of the heavy workload. Alarm after alarm comes that the tight financial framework and increased political governance combined with the high workload mean that the risk of breaking the law is imminent. Those who pay the highest price are those who live in social or economic vulnerability. People who, for various reasons, live under very difficult conditions are in danger of not getting the help they are entitled to. At worst, people's lives are at stake. From having previously been needs-driven, the work of social services has now become more and more performance-driven. The enormous demands placed on individuals today are based on outdated ideas that the socially vulnerable have not made sufficient efforts and are in fact trying to embed our systems. At the same time, trust in the skills of social workers has been replaced by solid control systems where efficiency is the goal, not that people should be helped to get back on their feet. Social work has had to stand back for market logic, where keeping the budget is the ultimate goal of social services. What does this say about our society, and what about our view of humanity? Placing the blame on the victim is a way for neoliberals to conceal the unfair distribution policies they pursue. The more you scoff at those who pay the price for their policies, the easier it is to break our security systems. This is also true of those who today are adults but were placed as children. We know that anyone who has been placed as a child is at high risk of mental illness as an adult. The municipalities have their own registers, but they look different in different places and have different quality. There is very little research in this area, but the studies available indicate that about a quarter of all notifications lead to efforts. It is a position which, among other things, is designed to prevent certain groups from being subjected to forced sterilisation. The right to become a parent applies to everyone, and people with cognitive difficulties are not and should not be excluded. However, people with cognitive disabilities often need more support. Young adults with cognitive difficulties, such as an intellectual disability, an acquired brain injury or a neuropsychiatric disability within the autism spectrum, such as Asperger, often do not have the ability to care for a child on their own. Many also find it difficult to accept the need for extra support, which affects both themselves and the children. When it comes to receiving support, it is a great responsibility for the individual himself to seek and receive help and support. We also see a high proportion of these children being taken care of and placed in community care. Today, there are no national guidelines to stick to when a person with cognitive difficulties is expecting a child. Some areas have come a long way in the work of municipalities and county councils. It is about both prevention and work during pregnancy and afterwards. There are examples of municipalities where you can borrow a computer doll and simply train yourself to take care of a child and test being a parent. It has turned out that it leads to fewer teenage pregnancies. There is very little research in this area, but there is research that shows that the right support for prospective parents increases the conditions for them to be able to take care of their children. But this help and support need to be available throughout the child's childhood. That also needs to change. There are those who can handle parenting, but not all. And there is currently nothing to regulate prevention. For children, this support needs to be available throughout childhood. Knowledge of what it means to have a parent with cognitive difficulties must be increased. This is particularly true in schools and preschools. Many times it is these children who end up outside the Community. They are bullied or find it difficult to pass school for various reasons. According to research, 42 percent of the children of parents with an intellectual disability were delayed in their own development without having their own intellectual disability. Since it is only when parents fail in their parental role that social services have a responsibility, it has often gone far too far before the children get the help they should have. Compulsory support should be available as soon as a pregnancy has been identified, but it is lacking. An estimate says that about 1,400 children of parents with cognitive difficulties are born each year, and somewhere between 40 and 60 percent of them are placed more or less directly in community care. These are children whose voices are never heard. Finally, as one of the first countries in the world, Sweden ratified the Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1990, and now finally the proposal to make it a bill has landed on the table of Parliament. The first and second Additional Protocols to the Convention on the Rights of the Child have also been ratified, but not the third Additional Protocol, which gives children an individual right of appeal, which is otherwise missing from the Convention on the Rights of the Child. This does not mean new rights for children, but it is a complementary tool for children to assert and enforce their rights on the basis of the Convention on the Rights of the Child. In practice, children are currently given limited opportunities to appeal against decisions concerning them, especially given that children must always have the consent of guardians for complaints. Children's rights must be strengthened in many areas, and the Convention on the Rights of the Child as a law is part of it. Of course, it is not possible to settle down. There's always more to do. Of course, I therefore coat the socks today – on the International Down Syndrome Day – to beat a blow for the equal value and rights of all people and to lift everyone's unique conditions. Of course, we Liberals support all our motions and reservations, but for time to come, I only support reservations 30, 32 and 50.

i-a3d72c2ab376980f-475	Madam President, I would like Financial stability is a question that for many people may seem difficult to see through to see what it really means, but it is the basis for the whole of our economy to work, for households to consume and for companies to invest – this for jobs to become more and for welfare to be financed. To this extent, financial stability is a very central issue for the Swedish parliament and for the whole country. There are many worrying signs here, Madam President, that the financial stability or resilience of the financial system may nevertheless fundamentally be threatened by a very poor policy of the current government, even if the banks are strong. There are underlying problems in society and major imbalances that have been built up. There has been a doubling of asset prices in a relatively short time when it comes to housing and the stock market. At the same time, we have seen an interest rate level that has been pushed down to abnormally or unknownly low levels. No one really knows what it means to have negative interest rates in an economy. In books on economics it is usually said that zero is the interest rate floor, the interest rate cannot be lower. Now we have a situation of negative interest rates, while house prices are running out and at the same time the stock world is running out. We do not really know what that means by extension. In this way they play from the overall politics, perhaps not Russian roulette but at least roulette, with stability and security in the Swedish economy. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-508	Mrs. Old President! Today we are debating the report by the Committee on Finance, Financial Stability and Financial Market Affairs. I shall refer to two Swedish democratic motions which affect this area in different ways. I'll start with exercise 547 Retirement pension contributions. Free competition is a prerequisite for an efficient market. If there are obstacles or restrictions to competition, whatever they may be, the risk is that the market will not function properly, and the cost will then be passed on to the consumer in the form of higher prices. That is exactly what has happened in the pension insurance market. It has been reported in the media that most life insurance companies have chosen to shock-raise the removal fees of certain pension insurance policies in order to lock in dissatisfied customers who would otherwise have moved their pension capital to other, more advantageous alternatives. There are currently no restrictions on how high the transfer fees pension companies can charge, and this possibility has, of course, been capitalised – at the expense of customers. The Competition Authority has analysed this very point in the report "Movement fees in the life insurance market", and their conclusions point in the same direction. The company notes that the removal fees involve a cost to the customer who wants to change insurance and move his pension capital to a competing company. Furthermore, the transfer fees for pension insurance have been increased by several life insurance companies in recent years in order to actively discourage customers from moving. To address the problems, the report proposes a number of changes to the Insurance Business Act and the Insurance Contracts Act. Among other things, it is proposed that the removal fee should cover the most necessary administrative costs, neither more nor less. It also proposes a time limit for how long after a new signature it should be possible to charge removal fees. In other words, the problem picture is clear, and it is unison. The only thing missing is political action to overcome this anomaly in the insurance market. As I understand it, work on this issue has been initiated, and there is work in progress at the Government Office to facilitate the transfer of pension savings. In a written reply, Minister Bolund has also promised regulatory changes in this area before the end of his term of office. I am therefore eagerly waiting for Parliament to receive the proposal in the near future. Mrs. Old President! Exercise 3671 addresses the housing problem in sparsely populated areas. In large parts of sparsely populated areas, it is difficult, sometimes impossible, to borrow to your home on the regular mortgage market. It can be difficult even if the applicant has a fixed income, is without payment notes and otherwise would more than be able to meet his commitments to the bank or the mortgage institution. The reasons may, of course, vary, but risks to the lender combined with poor profitability of small mortgages are often mentioned. Moreover, if there are few or no local bank accounts in the area, it makes it even more difficult, as no one really knows the local market with normally relatively few items. The difficulty of obtaining mortgages in rural areas has a number of negative consequences. One problem is that people with otherwise healthy economies are referred to expensive short-term loans with excessive loan costs as a result. But it also has negative effects on the entire local housing market. The items that come out for sale receive fewer interested speculators than they would otherwise have had, which in itself gives a lower selling price. Lower selling prices lower the value of surrounding properties, reducing residents' willingness to invest in maintenance, renovation and extensions. New constructions are very rare in these areas. The Swedish Democrats see a solution to the problem. The State owns a mortgage institution, SBAB, which has been imposed by the Government on a yield requirement similar to that of the rest of the market and which is largely similar to that of the private competitors. The purpose of the State's ownership of a mortgage institution is questionable from this point of view. Instead, the government should ensure that SBAB offers mortgages to individuals throughout Sweden through its control of SBAB. Any additional administrative and risk costs may be calculated and charged by the mortgage institution through, among other things, setup fees and interest rates. The government should review the yield requirement for SBAB in order to stimulate a functioning housing market also in sparsely populated areas. Of course, SBAB otherwise will do a regular credit rating and waive when the person is not considered creditworthy. I call for reservations 8 and 12.

One might think that one should have the right to decide over one's own money, and I think the vast majority of people agree with that. The strange thing is that the banks have taken that opportunity away from us. Today, three of the four major banks together have only 25 bank accounts in the whole country where cash is handled. Add to that that we are also at the bottom of the league in Europe when it comes to access to ATMs. It is quite obvious that one can easily see what problems it causes in society. Of course, it is good that cash handling is reduced. This leads to a lower risk of robbery and a reduction in the transport of banknotes and coins. But it is also the case that the number of card frauds is very large. In any case, we suspect it, but we do not really know, because the banks are not forced to account for the number of card frauds. Furthermore, many small businesses face problems with the reduction in cash handling. Many small businesses in rural areas are witnessing problems in getting rid of their day-to-day money. I myself have met entrepreneurs who testify that they are allowed to spend several days on the day's cash register because it is so expensive to get rid of it. But it is also very expensive to get change. I've met entrepreneurs who have had to travel over 100 miles to get rid of the day's cashier. You can imagine what it's like to work the whole long day, maybe 12 to 14 hours a day, and then get in the car and darn away 50 miles back and 50 miles home just to get rid of the money you earned. In addition, the pensioners' organisations, many of whom we are probably in this House who have been courted by, testify to the problems their members have when they cannot use cash. Pensioners' associations have problems getting rid of the bingo fund when they have bingo at the retirement home. They have to go very far to deposit the money in a bank. Old people sit on the bus or in the car with the bingo cash or the proceeds from the lottery. I've met sports clubs that testify to problems when they've been selling bingo tickets outside the store. It is very expensive to deposit your own money in the bank or get access to it once you withdraw it. But I have also met people who, for reasons of privacy, actually do not want to use cards, because they do not want their purchases to be registered and able to be used for other purposes, such as advertising or other forms of influence. After all, it's our own money. Once you have deposited your money in the bank, you should also be allowed to decide whether you can withdraw it yourself as cash. The Left Party has been pushing the issue of access to cash for many years now. I think it's about seven years. There are a great many people who have witnessed during these years the problems that the reduced cash management has led to. We tried to influence the bourgeois government during their time, but it was very difficult. Finally, the County Administrative Board of Dalarna was commissioned to produce an annual report in which they would describe what had happened during the year with access to cash management in the countryside. A little money was also set aside for the Swedish Post and Telecom Agency to be able to support rural traders. This government has increased that pot, because the Left Party pushed it through in the budget negotiations, and that is a good thing. But we all know that there is no solution to the problem. The cash management of banks must be regulated. I guess that's where the core lies. You don't dare take the fight with the banks. However, we in the Left Party believe that there is a need for a change of law, which obliges banks to maintain cash management. Mrs. Old President! In rural areas, the supply of capital may be problematic, not least for small businesses. There, the local savings banks play a very important role. Their knowledge of the local community enables them to make different positions than the major commercial banks. The task of the savings banks is, of course, to benefit the local community in which they operate and not above all to pay dividends to their owners, as is the case with the four major banks. Therefore, more local projects and more local companies should have had good access to capital where the local savings banks are located. But we also believe that it is totally unreasonable for local banks to compete on the same terms with the big giants. Today they have the same regulatory burden as the large commercial banks. This should be reviewed. We also believe that small banks should be better able to broaden their own funds. Therefore, government credit guarantees should be provided for, for example, the start-up of new local banks. The Left Party therefore proposes to set up a bank structure inquiry to strengthen bank competition and to increase the presence of smaller banks in rural areas in various ways. State SBAB has started to liquidate its lending in large parts of the country on the grounds that they prefer to be in the big cities. We believe that a state bank should be able to take responsibility for the whole country. Otherwise, the talk that we must hold the whole country together and provide good conditions for living and living in the whole country becomes quite hollow on the part of the state. Finally, I would like to address the issue of aggressive tax planning and tax evasion. We believe that banks that regularly contribute to aggressive tax schemes should have their permits withdrawn. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-533	Mrs. Old President! Safeguarding financial stability is a key task of the political system. Stable public finances and financial stability are crucial for jobs, security and well-being. We Social Democrats therefore take these tasks very seriously and with great vigour. During this term of office, we have once again turned large inherited deficits in public finances into robust surpluses. We have started to strengthen the financial system and have taken measures that can contribute to a more balanced development of the housing market. Basically, these issues are about people's ability to rely long-term and lasting trust that welfare is there and that there is a job to go to. If there is anything we know, it is that when there is no order in the economy and when financial stability is turned into its opposite, instability, then it is job, welfare and security for people who are affected. We also know that the work on securing financial stability is not over. However, we have a high level of preparedness, several new tools and powerful economic muscles – with a very low public debt as a good example – which means that we have the resources to do more when or if necessary. It's taking responsibility for the future. Mrs. Old President! As I have made clear, the consequences of financial crises are often very serious. In Sweden, perhaps the main vulnerability of the financial system is that we have a large, concentrated banking sector that operates in a small currency area and has a large share of market funding. When it links the development of household indebtedness, it is clear that this is an area that requires our attention. For a very long time, we have seen house prices and household indebtedness rising at a high rate, a trend that means that macro-economic risks can be built up and that there are risks that it will eventually be harmful to Sweden. When years have been added without progress being slowed down, the need to deal with the situation has become more evident. Efforts for increased housing construction and higher demands on banks are two important structural efforts, but we have also seen a clear need to stand up for a robust amortisation culture. Almost two years ago, Finansinspektionen therefore introduced the first amortisation requirement, and now in March it was further strengthened. The basic principle of repayment of debts should be upheld by the regulatory framework under which the financial sector is governed. Similarly, it is reasonable for the authorities to be able to require that loans with a high debt ratio be repaid more quickly than loans with a low debt ratio. The measures taken have broad support, as the report shows, for example, by the Riksbank and the IMF. Mrs. Old President! During this term of office, attention has also been paid to how the financial system has been used to round up the laws and regulations that apply in Sweden. It is about money that has been laundered to conceal criminal activities or the financing of terrorism. It is also about money being shoveled around between companies and in groups in order not to be taxed. That must not be the case, and that is not the way it should be. Critical activities and terrorism shall be combated. Income earned in Sweden shall be taxed in Sweden. It is essential for our country to stick together. The government has taken measures to counteract such exploitation of the financial system. We have tabled a new law to prevent the financing of terrorism. We have strengthened the rules on access to money laundering, and we have a comprehensive programme and work on the ground to prevent tax evasion. Together with other countries both within the EU and in the OECD, work continues to strengthen welfare tax bases and prevent leakage, fraud and crime. Mrs. Old President! We know that it is not enough to appeal to people's tax standards. There must also be robust tools and regulations in place. A well-functioning financial system is, of course, not only important at some kind of social level. In addition, almost all citizens and small businesses are consumers of financial services, and therefore also in their everyday lives depend on it working well from a consumer's perspective. However, the market for financial services and advice is characterised by information asymmetry. A party namely the bank, the insurance company, a seller or intermediary usually knows much more about the service than the one to buy it and may have completely different motives than the consumer benefit. In order to prevent the negative effects it may have, the Government has given priority to strengthening the position of consumers on the market. Among other things, rules for investment advice and information have been tightened up. It is clear that consumer protection is central to safeguarding competition and financial stability. The initiatives taken by the government but also the authorities and the European Union have been very welcome. Mrs. Old President! The issue of cash has been mentioned earlier in the debate. Over the past few years, we have seen how digitalisation has progressed faster and faster, not least when it comes to payment services. The banking and financial sector have in many ways taken the lead in the digital race, but they also leave many people behind. From the motions received, there are many of us in this Assembly, in the Swedish Parliament, who are concerned that more and more people are finding it difficult to manage their personal finances when the analogue alternatives, as they are almost described today, are disappearing in different ways. The Government has ensured that the issue of cash handling does not end up between the chairs through the Riksbank Committee, where several members of the Finance Committee contribute with work. We are tasked with investigating the matter. The work of the Committee is now ongoing. I am convinced that we will be able to find good solutions that will ensure that the cash remains and remains available in the future. In conclusion, I would like to say that during the past parliamentary term, the Socialist-led government has taken on key issues of financial stability. In addition to turning large government deficits into substantial surpluses and very low public debt, we have taken measures in the area of macro-stability and household indebtedness and, not least, housing construction. We have more and better tools to prevent the financial system from being exploited by criminals and terrorists. Consumer protection in the financial services market has been strengthened. Investigations have been set up to take a holistic approach to many major and important issues such as the EU Banking Union and the cash issue just mentioned. I therefore call for the committee's proposal in the report to be approved and for all reservations to be rejected.

i-a3d72c2ab376980f-669	Mrs. Old President! The system of guardians, good men and managers is part of society's safety net for the most vulnerable in society. Persons who, due to their age or illness, need good men or managers must be able to rely on the deputy to perform adequate work and that the supervision of the administration is adequate. Recruitment problems, lack of legal certainty, insufficient control, lack of central coordination and support, uneven education and knowledge in the country's municipalities are part of the shortcomings of the current system. Therefore, at the committee's proposal, in spring 2017, the Riksdag issued an announcement to the Government to take the initiative as soon as possible to monitor the functioning of the legislation in this area and to consider other improvements to the system of good men and managers. In order to overcome existing shortcomings and ensure a high level of quality in operations, it is now high time for a review of the entire legislation on the system of good governance and guardians. Such a thorough review is also recommended by the Swedish National Audit Office in its review report of December 2017 on the supervision of deputies and transfer guardians. A study should be set up with the task of drafting new legislation that better responds to current needs and conditions in a legally secure manner. The investigation should consider whether to introduce a purely municipal responsibility for good men and managers within their own organisation. Proposals for legislative amendments should be made to introduce joint and several municipal liability for property offences committed by a deputy against the principal in the context of his mandate. For the Left Party, it is a fundamental starting point that an individual who, for various reasons, receives support from society should not suffer financial damage from this, but in this respect be kept free of harm. We therefore believe that legal protection needs to be improved and that there should be increased possibilities for the principal to obtain compensation from the municipality for damage caused by the deputy. It is also appropriate to propose that all deputies undergo compulsory basic training and that the transfer guardian be responsible for providing them with the training necessary to carry out the mission as well as proposals on the organisation of training for transfer guardians and transfer guardian boards to ensure a higher level of competence among them. The Left Party wants to see better coordination, cooperation and information for deputies and for those who are willing to take a position as deputy. For example, there could be a telephone service or a digital service where deputies could turn for support, help and information. Furthermore, a common forum for all regulated voluntary assignments can facilitate support for contractors and answers to questions arising during ongoing assignments, such as the current regulatory framework. The government should therefore set up an investigation tasked with reviewing the system of good men for unaccompanied minors with a view to introducing a maximum limit on waiting time in order to obtain a good man and to ensure a high level of quality in the performance of the mission. Another issue that needs further examination is the support from good men to children with special needs. This can be about children with needs that require special skills to help properly. There is generally a great value in the fact that the mission as a good man is carried out by volunteers and not by employed persons. For children with special needs, however, there may be reason to explore the possibility of introducing a model with professional men. The most important thing is to ensure that these children receive the support they need and that their good men have the necessary skills. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-724	Mrs. Old President! After what follows from the 5th chapter. Section 1 of the Old Railway Act is to be used by the Finnish Transport Agency as a starting point to compensate for the damage caused by railway operations, so-called strict liability. This strict liability means that the Finnish Transport Administration normally pays compensation for certain relatives of third parties who have died in rail transport without actually making a difference to whether this is an accident or a suicide. According to Trafikverket, such compensation is intended to cover, on the one hand, compensation for funeral expenses and, on the other hand, compensation for non-profit damage to certain relatives with a flat-rate amount of a price base. This is good. One could say that the Swedish Transport Administration assumes responsibility for the fact that an unauthorized person is in the track and is hit by a car. On the other hand, the new railway law, as it is written, does not provide compensation for non-profit damage for, for example, mental suffering to the driver who is driving on a person. The Swedish Transport Administration suddenly assumes no responsibility for the fact that an unauthorized person has been on the track. The engineer who, against his will, has killed another person, which is always a traumatic experience that persecutes the driver for the rest of the driver's life, receives no compensation at all from the Finnish Transport Administration. In order to receive compensation, the train driver is forced after a couple of months to a degrading investigation with the insurance company where one should report on the traumatic experience again. The insurance agent has no training in crisis management. The insurance agent's job is to save money for his company, but the agent only tears up old wounds in the engineer. If, after a few months, Trafikverket had called a relative of the person who killed himself – a mother, father, widower, widower or a child – and asked how you feel two months after Dad killed himself in front of the train, or if you can sleep at night, or if you think about it about the days, would that have been acceptable? Of course not. Of course, the driver should not have these conversations, just as family members do not. Mrs. Old President! Many years ago I drove a freight train one Sunday morning from Nässjö down to Malmö. Suddenly, at a red car, there's an old lady standing right in front of the track. She's walking towards me. Against everything I've learned, to hold your ears and close your eyes, I make eye contact with man. Just before I hit her, she throws herself at the locomotive, just like she's going to push it. She's going under the locomotive. I'll run her over with the whole freight train and stop. I'll call the train traffic control. They, in turn, alert the emergency services. The rescue service comes in and examines the train and comes up to me and says: We can't find anything. Then I'll start thinking about pink elephants. I describe an elderly woman in her 70's to 75's, in a gray jacket, who threw herself in front of the locomotive. Oh, the cat, they say, she's the one who's in the car behind the train. We thought it was strange that there's a woman sitting on a Sunday morning just waiting. They ran back, but the woman had driven from the scene. I'm waiting for our peer supporter. We have a fantastic peer support organization in the railway that takes care of us after such an accident. When they arrive at the same time the train traffic is released on, and the first train that passes is the sleeping train that comes from Stockholm. It passes for 40 kilometers an hour. After a couple of minutes, the train traffic control calls me up and asks if the peer supporter has arrived. Yes, he had arrived. Then we were told that we could drive to the next station because there had been another accident, they said. Let's get in the cab and go to the next station. When we get there, I see the red car. When we get out of the cab, I see the body lying on the embankment. The woman has laid her neck on the rails. After two months, the insurance company calls me and asks me how I feel etcetera. I managed that event pretty well. I convinced myself that perhaps the woman did this because she had cancer or for some other reason. After all, she was old. I had to tell you how I slept, how I felt about it, if I thought about the event, blah blah blah and so on. Then I received a compensation of SEK 3,000. The funny thing about this, Madam President, is that my fellow engineer who actually drove the woman to death got a thousand less. A couple of years ago, it was time again. Then there was a girl in her 18 to 20s who threw herself in front of the train when I came driving. The same organization started again, the police came, the rescue services came, and the comrades came in. Then there was crisis management after that. When I went home after the debriefing, conversations, and so on, Madam Old President, I called my daughter, who was then 20 years old. My daughter replied: Hello, Dad! Then it broke completely for me. I thought there were two parents, a mother and a father, who today do not have a daughter to call and who answer: Hello, Dad! This incident has haunted me virtually every day. There won't be a day without me thinking about this. Two months later, the insurance company calls me. A busy woman introduces herself and starts asking me: How are you today? Are you thinking about the experience? Can you sleep at night? It was a hell of a hearing! It ended up, Madam Old President, that I actually asked her to go to a certain place down. I think it would have been some kind of recognition from the Finnish Transport Administration if the same rules had applied to us train drivers as to the relatives. Mrs. Old President! I'm talking here for all 3,000 train drivers in the country. Let's not have to tear up old wounds. Let's just move on. We are as much affected as the relatives, and we should have the same rights as the relatives. The country's train driver, Madam President-in-Office, calls on all parties here to support our reservation. I commend our reservation. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-742	Mrs. Old President! Negotiations on a comprehensive EU-Canada Trade and Investment Agreement started in 2009. Five years later, the CETA agreement was presented. Apart from the fact that the agreement must be approved by the Council of Ministers of the European Union and the European Parliament, as has already been done, it must also be approved at national level in all the parliaments of the Member States in order for the agreement to apply in all parts. Today is the debate we have in the Swedish Parliament. Sweden is a small export-dependent country. Our trade with the outside world is crucial to our prosperity and development. Over the years, the Left Party has actively supported the abolition of customs duties, quotas and other formal barriers to trade by Sweden. Today, the EU and Sweden have already phased out many of their tariffs and quotas against large economies and important trading partners. Therefore, the criticism of the CETA agreement is not about tariffs and quotas, but about the fact that the agreement imposes democratic restrictions, a deterioration of social rights, difficulties in regulating welfare and obstacles to environmental and climate measures. The agreement has also been strongly criticised for the lack of democratic transparency in the negotiation process. Unnecessaryly, the agreement unfortunately means a shift of power to large companies as it offers unique opportunities for them to sue Sweden and other EU countries. The Left Party is neither opposed to trade nor advocates of trade barriers. We want a fair trade policy that does not mean any deterioration for the citizens of the countries covered. We want a trade policy that puts people, animals and the environment first and focuses on increased social benefits, not just for big business. That is why we are today rejecting the Government's proposal to approve the CETA agreement. For Sweden, Canada is a very small trading partner. Exports to Canada account for 0.8 percent of Sweden's total exports and imports for 0.3 percent of total imports. Canada is 37th in the ranking of the countries that Sweden imports most from and at 23rd place over the countries that Sweden exports most to. The supporters of CETA have high economic expectations of the agreement. At the same time, studies show that the agreement will bring relatively small economic benefits. There are also studies showing that there may be negative macroeconomic effects, partly because of job losses and partly because of the strengthening of existing economic gaps. There are thus differences of opinion as to the extent of the impact of the agreement. It has also proved unclear with other trade agreements. The effects are extremely complex and complex to predict, something that has been shown time and again. Indeed, it is not unlikely that CETA will bring some financial gain to the parties. The question, however, is how big it will be, who will have access to it and, not least, whether it is proportionate to the risks arising from the agreement. CETA, like the criticised TTIP previously planned between the EU and the US, includes a chapter on regulatory cooperation. The aim of regulatory cooperation is to avoid existing and future unnecessary barriers to trade and investment and avoid unnecessary regulatory differences. For this purpose, a regulatory cooperation forum shall be established within CETA to evaluate legislative initiatives. This may sound laudable, but in practice it means that a non-politically elected assembly will examine political proposals in advance from a trade perspective. Although the agreement contains safeguard clauses which ensure that countries' right to regulate and legislate is not restricted by regulatory cooperation, there is an imminent risk that it will act as a blocking body for progressive legislation, for example in the field of the environment. So it has worked with the American OIRA, which has been the model of the proposal. It will also be relevant to ask who is allowed to define what constitutes a barrier to trade. A national ban on fossil fuels in favour of renewable energy can either be regarded as an acute necessity for the planet's survival or as a barrier to trade, depending on who is allowed to decide. One of the most controversial parts of CETA is investor protection. The aim is to ensure that investors who feel that a State is in breach of the agreement can appeal to a special court. This means that investors are given the right to sue states, i.e. Sweden, other EU countries and Canada, if legislation and regulations can be considered to have a negative impact on the value of the investment. In plain language, companies can demand compensation from states for loss of profits. Within the framework of CETA, a court of 15 judges shall be appointed. The lawsuit and the legal process are thus taking place in this court outside the national legal system. ICS is a further development of a similar system found in many other trade agreements called ISDS. However, ISDS has received a lot of criticism, and the mechanisms in CETA are therefore slightly modified but based on the same principles. ISDS protects the investor against direct expropriation. Today, however, ISDS has increasingly become a protection against so-called indirect expropriation. It is therefore no longer a matter of protecting assets owned by companies, but of protecting their estimated profits. Thus, companies can sue states if they consider that national legislation prevents them and their ability to make profits. ISDS has thereby become an effective means of preventing progressive legislation and, by extension, constitutes a direct threat to our democracy. Since both EU Member States and Canada have stable and functioning legal systems, there is no apparent reason to bring disputes in this type of new court. Trade agreements between States with functioning legal systems should therefore not contain ISDS clauses or similar mechanisms. On the one hand, the need is lacking and, on the other, the ordinary legal system is circumvented. Moreover, democratic decisions are at risk of being undermined or even lacking due to the profit interests of companies. Furthermore, there is no clear evidence that the amount of investment flows would benefit from ICS. In other words, the benefits are very unclear, but the risks are all the greater. Moving political decisions from national parliaments to a set of lawyers is a serious matter, not least from a democratic point of view. Moreover, a very worrying effect is that states themselves seem to adapt their legislation in order to avoid being sued. One of the more well-known examples is when the tobacco company Philip Morris launched a lawsuit against Australia in 2011 based on the Hong Kong-Australia investment agreement. The reason was that the country wanted to introduce neutral cigarette packs without advertising or pictures in order to reduce smoking. Philip Morris claimed that the legislation involved a so-called indirect expropriation. Despite the fact that Australia won after a lengthy legal process, the lawsuit still had the consequence that New Zealand decided to wait for similar legislation until the verdict had come to an end. Also Swedish Vattenfall's two lawsuits of Germany have mainly been about stopping and worsening new legislation rather than getting monetary compensation. The criticism is in many ways similar to that which existed against the TTIP agreement. There have been concerns as to how the agreement could lead large multinational US companies to use their resources to pressure EU countries to counter trade union rights and environmental requirements and reduce welfare. CETA opens the back door so that US subsidiaries with operations in Canada also have the opportunity to sue European states. Today, one in ten companies in Canada is American-owned. How is Sweden affected by ICS? According to a report by UNCTAD, there has been a sharp increase in ISDS cases since the early 2000s. North American large companies are over-represented in bringing states to justice in order to secure estimated profits and investment values. One current area is the mining sector. Sweden is a relatively attractive country for mineral prospectuses, and many Canadian companies are already operating in Sweden. The mining industry is a strategically important industry, not least in regions that have previously suffered from high unemployment and emigration. At the same time mining has a major impact on the environment and environment. Against new exploitations are many times other interests. In Sweden, the compensation to state and property owners in the event of mining is very small in an international comparison. It amounts to only 2 per mil of the estimated value of the minerals that have been mined. According to statistics from the World Bank, Sweden's mining industry has the lowest taxation of all mining countries in the world. According to the statistics, the effective tax rate amounts to approximately 28 per cent. In very few counties, the tax rate is less than 40 per cent. Mining has been an area where many lawsuits have taken place. Canadian companies are currently involved in extractive activities in Spain, Ireland, Finland, Romania, Bulgaria and Greece, where they have been accused of not taking environmental considerations into account. Many times mining projects have been carried out in sensitive natural areas and have destroyed the conditions for local tourism business. The local population has also not been taken into account, and the discharges of mines have affected local agriculture. The Canadian company Glamis Gold sued California when the state imposed restrictions on restoring an area for their mine taking into account the indigenous population. Fortunately, the company lost that particular case. Another example is the Canadian mining company Gabriel Resources, which sued Romania for billions after Parliament rejected a controversial mining project for environmental reasons. For Sweden, CETA would provide an opportunity for the Canadian mining industry to sue the state every time Swedish authorities impose environmental restrictions or want to take account of the local population and its industries. The Left sees a great need for a change in mineral legislation, which takes greater account of the municipalities concerned, the environment and the climate. However, there is considerable doubt as to whether such a change in legislation is in line with the commitments in CETA. With the agreement in place, it will not be the national parliaments and courts who will interpret it, but it will be handed over to the ICS Court. Another example is the question of limiting profits in welfare. The Left Party has long been pushing that private companies should not be able to fill the treasure chest by impoverishing our common welfare. The support for a welfare without profits is great in Sweden, and as many as 74 percent would like to see a stop. Despite uncertainties as to how CETA may affect Swedish welfare, the government chooses not to take criticism seriously. Measures at municipal level may also be subject to the investment protection of the agreement. If you decide to ensure good quality in your welfare, CETA can provide a basis for a lawsuit if this is considered to have a negative impact on the company's trade and investments. In the bill, the government states that the CETA agreement does not have any negative fiscal consequences. This is a strange assumption given that Sweden can be sued for billions of dollars. The Danish Government has acknowledged that the CETA agreement may have financial consequences if one is sued as a country. Therefore, the government should investigate the financial consequences of the CETA agreement and return to the Riksdag with an analysis. There are different ways of establishing trade agreements. As regards the agreements previously concluded by the EU, a so-called positive list of services covered is normally used. This means listing a list of services to be subject to liberalisation commitments under the agreements. In the case of CETA, the EU is now entering into an agreement with the opposite model for the first time, using a negative list. In that case, all services that are not clearly excluded are covered. Liberalisation thus becomes the rule. In Sweden, the liberalisation of many important markets has led to reduced accessibility and service. This has been the case, for example, for the pharmacy market, which has reduced the availability of medicines for several vulnerable groups. Another example is the liberalisation of the electricity market, which has led to significantly higher tariffs for customers. So far, it is difficult to find out what the actual differences will be in practice. However, CETA is still a trend break, and without further analysis this system is approved, which may seem unfortunate. It cannot be ruled out that the change can affect the political room for manoeuvre in relation to Swedish liberalisations. CETA also means that liberalisation in the field of services will not be able to reverse. This applies regardless of what the political majority wishes. For example, if Sweden chooses to privatise new markets, such as Systembolaget, a so-called ratchet clause is automatically activated, which prevents a restored monopoly, whether such privatisation could prove to be very poor from a public health point of view or be a bad deal. The question is therefore settled in advance by the agreement. In conclusion, there are a number of reasons for concern about CETA and the consequences of this agreement. For Sweden, for example, it can be about our independence when it comes to deciding on important climate measures, about our mining industry and about privatisations and sales. Sweden depends on a good export. But it must not be at the expense of important social rights or opportunities to halt climate change. With the Treaty of Lisbon, power was transferred from Member States to the European Commission in negotiating trade agreements. But trade policy remains a partly national matter. It is clear that many in Europe are critical of the CETA agreement. CETA has been negotiated without further Swedish discussion or debate, even though the agreement has extensive implications for trade in areas that affect many people. CETA has been negotiated behind closed doors, and opportunities for elected politicians to influence have been small. The Left Party wants to see a trade policy that takes advantage of social and trade union rights, which safeguard democracy, gender equality and human rights, and which is based on sustainability and long-term sustainability. The CETA agreement is not an agreement that is in line with such a policy. The Swedish Parliament should therefore reject the Government's proposal to approve the CETA agreement between the EU and Canada. Mrs. Old President! Two weeks ago, the Court of Justice ruled that BITS-type investment agreements within the EU are not compatible with EU law. The clarification of the Court of Justice of the European Union now concerns the investment part of the BITS agreement between the Netherlands and Slovakia. In the long run, this means that all 196 bilateral investment agreements between countries within the EU must be abolished, of which Sweden today has eight. It is also on the table of the Court of Justice of the European Union to determine whether the investment component of the CETA agreement with Canada is compatible with EU law. Some countries have now announced that they will await this verdict before proceeding with the ratification process of the CETA agreement. It is important to remember that several recent judgments of the European Court of Justice on trade policy have given the Commission back homework. If a Member State enters into an agreement with a third country, although the Member State can reasonably foresee that the agreement is contrary to EU law, the Member State acts in breach of the principle of fair cooperation in the EU Treaty. Sweden would therefore act in breach of the EU Treaty if Parliament today ratified the CETA agreement before the EU Court of Justice has given its opinion. Therefore, on behalf of the Left Party, I call for the entire report to be referred back to the Foreign Affairs Committee for further consideration until it is clear whether the CETA agreement is in breach of EU law. The proposal for referral back must be considered before Parliament deals with the content of the report itself.

i-a3d72c2ab376980f-823	Mr President, I would like to The Green Party is fundamentally in favour of dismantling unnecessary barriers to trade between states.
