Perhaps it is most interesting to look at what is said about investor protection. In this respect, the National Board of Trade has concluded that the CETA agreement's provisions on the right to compensation in the event of indirect expropriation do not, in all material respects, provide any protection that goes beyond the protection already guaranteed to investors investing in Sweden. Investment protection does not affect Sweden's ability to maintain monopolies and utilities. If Sweden has allowed an investment and then introduces or reintroduces a monopoly, this may, under special conditions, form the basis for the liability of the State, as already provided for in Swedish law. However, if the monopoly can be justified by the general good, the risk of having to pay compensation is less. The college considered that the protection of investment had no effect on Sweden's right to regulate in the areas analysed. This is important to remember. The clarification here is that CETA, among other things, will not mean that any Swedish laws need to be amended. In addition to this, I think it is important to emphasise that the rules on what counts as indirect expropriation are rather strict. That's a lot for a state to be liable for. Among other things, there are a number of criteria that need to be met. Is the dispute based on an existing investment? Was the government's measure permanent in time? Did the investor expect the measure? Did the government have an honest purpose? Was the State's measure discriminatory? Was the purpose of the state measure a legitimate public welfare objective? If the purpose was a legitimate public welfare objective, was the measure manifestly excessive? There are therefore a number of criteria that make it much more for a State to be liable for compensation. This is important to remember, and it has responded to some of the objections that have been raised. One can of course choose to be sceptical about the conclusions of the National Board of Trade, but remember that the National Board of Trade is not the only ones who have landed in this conclusion. Here is one of the most important votes that comes from the Chancellor of Justice, who is actually the legal representative of the state: According to the Chancellor of Justice's assessment, the investment protection contained in CETA essentially corresponds to the protection that already exists in other international agreements and conventions that Sweden has taken up in Union law and in Swedish law. So it is not just the National Board of Trade that lands in this conclusion. These answers have been reassuring to us in the Green Party, because we have not wanted an agreement that jeopardises EU law or the democratically made decisions that exist in Sweden and will continue to exist in the future. With this, Mr President, I support the committee's proposal.
i-a3d72c2ab376980f-837	Mr President, I would like to With an agreement that has such a clear unilateral trading perspective as CETA, it will be very difficult for countries to develop their environmental and climate legislation. Countries need to have the courage to take the lead in achieving our climate goals, as decided in the Paris Agreement. Instead, the CETA agreement risks becoming a major brake block for the countries' environmental and climate efforts. There are examples of large companies using investor protection in trade agreements to sue states for their environmental policies. For example, in November 2015, the Obama administration in the United States decided to stop a Canadian oil pipeline through the United States as it was expected to lead to increased greenhouse gas emissions. The company that had planned the construction, Trans Canada, responded by suing the Obama administration for billions on the basis of NAFTA's investor protection. Other examples where investor protection has been used to sue states for environmental legislation include the closure of environmentally harmful trade by the Canadian state of Quebec and Ecuador's decision to stop oil extraction in highly sensitive natural areas. The ICS mechanism in CETA operates in a similar way, allowing large companies to over-examine democratically agreed decisions to protect nature or reduce emissions. In addition, the indirect impact of environmental regulations and progressive legislation is added. One example is how Canada has already pressed the EU to soften the classification of fuel from Canadian tar sands. It is therefore a direct consequence of a desire for increased trade between Canada and the EU, which is very likely to lead to greater consumption of dirty fuel in the EU. Several stakeholders have criticised the CETA agreement for the fact that the mining, energy and transport sectors lack wording on sustainability. The French Government has requested a climate veto in the CETA agreement. It calls for clarification in the agreement which states that no company should be able to sue states for measures aimed at halting global warming. No such demand has been heard from the Swedish Government. Why would the CETA agreement be good for the environment, Lorentz Tovatt? Aren't you worried?

i-a3d72c2ab376980f-889	Mr President, I would like to Sweden and the world need more free trade, not less. It is also about our relations with a very similar-minded country with which Sweden already has very good relations, namely Canada. The proposal concerns two agreements between the EU and its Member States and Canada. The first is the Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement, the CETA Agreement. The second is the Strategic Partnership Agreement, which includes general commitments and which develops cooperation and political dialogue with Canada in a number of important areas, such as international peace and security, the promotion of human rights, sustainable development and judicial cooperation. The CETA negotiations started in June 2009. In October 2017, the Agreement was signed. Parts of the Agreement have been provisionally applied since 21 September 2017. So far, seven EU Member States – Latvia, Denmark, Malta, Croatia, the Czech Republic, Estonia and Spain – have notified the European Commission that they have ratified CETA. If Parliament decides in plenary today or tomorrow, Sweden will be the eighth country of the EU's 28 member states to support CETA. CETA is a modern and progressive trade and investment agreement that will deepen the economic and trade relationship between the EU and Canada. It's a modern deal. It takes into account trends in international trade, such as the increasing importance of global value chains and growing trade in services. This can be done by the fact that the agreement is comprehensive and not only relates to customs facilitation but also to technical barriers to trade, customs and trade procedures, intellectual property, investment and investment protection and voluntary regulatory cooperation. The agreement is progressive, as it concerns rules on trade and sustainable development and work. Mr President, I would like to For our companies, the CETA agreement is welcome and requested. Although Canada is the world's tenth largest economy, it is only Sweden's 24th largest trading partner. There's potential here. The CETA agreement will increase trade. Global competitive pressure is increasing while Swedish industry is becoming increasingly intertwined in global value chains and knowledge networks. This applies to both large and small businesses in cities as well as in rural areas. This shows over 2,300 exporting companies in Sweden exporting to Canada. About 21 000 jobs are created in Sweden through our exports to Canada today. Sweden and Swedish companies have a good reputation in Canada, and Swedish companies show great interest in Canada as a market. The agreement has great potential for Swedish companies, and there is a great deal of interest in Canada. The number of companies registered to benefit from the lower duties has more than tripled – from 75 to 260 – since the provisional entry into force of the Agreement in September. The agreement thus abolishes customs duties on goods and reduces barriers to trade. This means a completely new market opening that benefits companies, especially small companies that cannot afford to adapt their production to different markets. The opportunities are increasing for Swedish companies to participate in the Canadian public procurement. Mr President, I would like to In my role as Minister of Trade, I regularly meet entrepreneurs who see opportunities, dare to think big and can take the step beyond our borders to be competitive in international markets. The agreement abolishes the 18% tariffs on their underwear and reinforcement clothing and makes them more competitive. CETA means for a larger Swedish agricultural cooperative savings of SEK 2 million in import duties every time they import the plastic film used for straw bales. The company Garden & Greenhouse in Östergötland imports glass houses and saves up to 10 000 SEK per delivery when importing glass houses. In terms of sustainable development, the environment and work, the provisions of the Agreement link it with the EU's overall sustainable development objectives and specific objectives in terms of labour, environment and climate change. In the social field, the parties have agreed that the agreement should not lead to a reduction in labour standards. Instead, it should work towards raising standards and actively work to implement the ILO acquis. All the requirements of the current legislation on labour and social protection continue to apply. This also includes rules on the right to strike and the possibility of negotiating collective agreements. CETA expressly states that wage dumping is rejected. Regarding environmental issues in CETA, the importance of multilateral environmental agreements and mutual support between trade, environmental policy, rules and measures is emphasised. Environmental legislation should not be disregarded in order to promote trade or investment. The right to regulate and also to increase protection in these areas is something that Canada and the EU have consistently agreed on. This is also inscribed in the agreement. CETA therefore does not prevent us from pursuing a progressive policy that puts people and the environment first. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-933	Mr President, I would like to I'll take the last question first, or I won't be able to. The Government has thoroughly examined the question of what the Court has said and concluded that there is no basis for the concerns expressed in Belgium's request. The judgment in that case, known as Achmea, does not alter that assessment. This is due to the situation of investment protection agreements between EU countries and CETA and the new court, called ICS. It differs in several ways, but above all on one crucial point: In the Achmea case, the Court of Justice found that the Slovak-Dutch Investment Protection Agreement meant that an arbitration tribunal under the agreement would interpret and apply EU law in a way that was not compatible with Union law. CETA states that a tribunal under this Agreement shall not have jurisdiction to interpret EU law. It is further stated that the CETA tribunal shall be obliged to comply with the advisory interpretation of EU law. Investor protection has, of course, been a controversial issue in the debate. That's why we've looked into it this closely. The second question was more about the investment agreement itself. It is one of the issues that the government has investigated most. It is about investment protection providing an investor with basic protection against discrimination and expropriation. What is stated in CETA is largely in line with Swedish law, and that is no longer possible. It is a protection that already exists today; it means both the National Board of Trade and the Chancellor of Justice. The dispute resolution mechanism cannot, therefore, reject laws or decisions that have been democratically taken.
i-TeGfbfn5VxM71FYnFgCEw2	Mr President, I would like to We are debating Economic and Trade Agreements ( CETA) and Strategic Partnership Agreements between the EU and its Member States and Canada. The CETA agreement is not a simple trade agreement. Had it been the case that the CETA agreement had been aimed at removing a few duties and tariffs and making it even easier to trade across the Atlantic, we in the Left Party would have thought it was a good proposal. But now the C in CETA stands for comprehensive. It is thus an all-embracing economic agreement between the EU and Canada. One could say that it is an agreement that has very great claims. It will cover almost all our policies. We have heard in some previous speeches, for example from the Green Party's Lorentz Tovatt– which I do not see in this House; I think that is a little strange – and from Minister Ann Linde, that there are positive wordings in the CETA agreement on the importance of increased political cooperation in, for example, the environment. It's important. What is less talked about is that the CETA agreement provides for further liberalisation, that it has the ambition that the whole transatlantic area should be opened up to public procurement, that in cap. 21 states that there should be a form of regulatory cooperation between Canada and the EU and EU Member States, and not least the controversial investor protection enshrined in the agreement. For this reason, we in the Left Party are critical of this agreement and are calling for a rejection of CETA. I would like to begin by calling for our reservation to be accepted on this point, reservation No 1. Mr President, I will not be able to go into all our criticisms of the CETA agreement. Much has been mentioned exemplaryly by the previous speaker from the Left Party, Håkan Svenneling, but I would like to say a few things about the areas I have raised. I start with investor protection, which you find developed mainly in cap. 8 of the CETA Agreement. Investor protection should provide investors, i.e. companies, with guarantees that their investments will bear fruit. Otherwise, one might think that the definition of an investment is that someone is taking a certain risk of obtaining currency for it in the future. However, the investor protection included in this agreement states, for example, that the companies should be protected against direct or indirect expropriation. I think it is deliberately formulated as direct or indirect expropriation – because it will be difficult to define exactly what it is. It is also said that companies should have fair and equitable treatment. There are a number of other criteria for how to build up investor protection. There is really nothing new about investor protection in international trade and investor agreements. It already exists in a variety of other agreements. I think perhaps the best and most comparable example is the NAFTA agreement between the USA, Canada and Mexico, which was concluded in 1994. Following the NAFTA agreement, we can see that Canada has become the most likely country in the world. American companies have taken every chance to sue Canadian lawmakers, particularly in the field of the environment and natural resources. Håkan Svenneling mentioned earlier Lone Pine Resources, who sued Canada when the state of Quebec introduced a regional ban on fracturing, i.e. blasting gas from the bedrock. It is an extremely environmentally harmful activity. As mentioned earlier, Canadian companies have also sued the US government; for example, Transcanada has sued the Obama government's decision to stop the great pipeline XL, which would go through the United States. We should also remember that Canadian companies have learned about investor protection. They have learned very well how the investor tribunals work, and they do not hesitate to sue European countries if they can. For example, the Canadian mining company Gabriel Resources did this: Romania was sued a number of years ago when the Romanian Parliament refused to allow Gabriel Resources to open a large gold mine in western Romania. I believe that Romania has now even classified the area as a protected area. Romania has been sued for the equivalent of SEK 40 billion. SEK 40 billion corresponds to half of Romania's entire health budget. This can be borne in mind when Romania says it cannot afford to take care of its poor people, and these people instead go to other countries to beg and do underpaid jobs. The Government Ann Linde says that with the CETA agreement we get a completely different approach and a completely different form of investor protection. I would like to ask the supporters of CETA what is really the big difference with the new investor protection in this agreement. You get a permanent court with 15 judges, but otherwise most of it is actually very similar to the current system we can see for investor protection in other agreements. It is a system that is completely outside the ordinary legal system, and one can sue on fluffy and unclear grounds. It is also, Lorentz Tovatt and others, only companies that can sue states. The states can't in reverse sue the corporations. I heard you talking about the fine wording that exists about the environment and climate in the CETA agreement and in the partnership agreement that is linked to this, Lorentz. These are very fine formulations, but what is their bearing capacity? Can we lean on those wordings and conversely sue companies that we believe are misbehaving towards the environment with regard to investor protection? No, it's not possible. It's just companies that can sue states. Ann Linde from the Social Democrats and Lorentz Tovatt from the Green Party say that this investor protection does not go beyond what is in our laws today. I would like to turn the question around a little bit. What's wrong with our regular judiciary today? Is it not the case that the EU, its Member States and Canada are the rule of law with functioning judicial systems to which one can turn if one as a company considers oneself badly treated by the state? You say in this agreement that it is incredibly important to trust Canadian authorities in other contexts, for example when it comes to the approval of various things that are going on the market. But just when it comes to the courts, we're going to have a separate corporate court system. Why, Lorentz Tovatt? I would also like to ask the Green Party and the Social Democrats: do you really believe in the utmost seriousness that Canadian mining companies will not take advantage of the chance to sue Sweden if, for example, we ban mining in certain areas or impose a mining tax of 10 % – something that the companies might judge as direct or indirect expropriation? Do you really seriously believe that investor protection cannot have a cooling effect on progressive legislation? Mr President, I would like to I would also like to say a few words about the so-called regulatory cooperation enshrined in Article 21 of the CETA Agreement. It may sound very good to cooperate on future rules and laws, but as the regulatory cooperation is designed, there should be committees within the framework of the CETA agreement that will examine proposals for new laws in advance. They shall examine the proposals from a trade perspective. Do the Green Party and the Social Democrats really believe that this regulatory cooperation will be used to strengthen, for example, labour law, environmental protection, welfare or other areas? The companies will have an obvious place in these regulatory cooperation committees, which will review all future legislation. Those of you who have read the proposal can also take note of the views of both the Swedish Chemicals Inspectorate and the Swedish Environmental Protection Agency on the regulatory cooperation. It is clearly warned that this may hamper future progressive legislation. Or, as they say in North America: This will be about paralyzing by analyzing – through demands for new analyses and new reports, progressive legislation will stop. In the time we live in, we need more progressive legislation. We need to make it easier to legislate in, for example, the environment – not more difficult. We in the Left Party also see many other reasons to say no to CETA. Håkan Svenneling of the Left Party referred earlier to the fact that deregulation is, as it were, the main rule of CETA and that it is extremely difficult to renationalise something that has already been privatised when there is a so-called ratchet clause in the agreement. It is also very unfortunate that the wording on patents and intellectual property – a very forgotten issue in the debate, I would say – goes much further than today's legislation in the EU and Canada. For example, the EU has been a driving force for extending the licence period for medicines, which will make the development of generic medicines more difficult. So it is medicines without patents that are cheaper for the population. It will also be more difficult to impose progressive requirements in public procurement when public procurement is to become part of a transatlantic market. I am also concerned about how we can carry out progressive animal protection in the future. Earlier today, I listened to the Chairman of Animal Justice Camilla Björkbom, who warned against the CETA agreement. It puts competition first and predicts that cross-border trade in beef will be tenfold, which will be a downward pressure on progressive legislation. It will be difficult for countries that want to take the lead in the field of animal welfare. This is Animal Justice's assessment of the CETA agreement. Mr President, I would like to Why does the European and North American environmental and trade union movements as well as animal rights organisations in Europe and Canada say no to CETA? I think it's as simple as they don't want a straitjacket on progressive politics. They want us to be able to take the lead in all these important policy areas. That's why we in the Left Party say no to CETA. I would also like to take this opportunity to support Mr Svenneling's and the Left Party's proposal to refer this matter back to committee. The judgment that came to the European Court of Justice two weeks ago is quite exceptional. It condemns investor protection between different EU countries – the same kind of investor protection that is now included in the CETA agreement. That is why we in the Left Party say: send the agreement back to committee so that we can analyse the verdict more closely! I apologise for having gone over time, Mr President, but this is a very important issue.

At best, environmental decisions and protection of health and public health are not made more difficult, but most likely so. Then we wonder what trade we can promote. Yes, it's the services. The customs duties are not a large part of the agreement. They are not the ones who have the economic impact; they are the services. That's where the details lie. But then there is the question of certain products. Do we really want to increase exports to Canada of machines to extract tar sands or more efficient forest machines that can cut down even more prime forests? Do we really want to sell gingerbread and buy brownies instead of changing recipes? Is that logical? Is it important now that we are trying to combat climate change? We are pleased that we are reducing emissions, but the reason is that we do not include the emissions of transport. Do we really want to increase transport and emissions? But both countries can say that emissions are falling in national statistics. Hunting for growth and increased trade in equivalent products – half of the world's trade takes place with equivalent products – is relatively pointless. The half dealing with different products can make sense. This agreement means for those who want growth at best 0.03% in one-off effect. But what's the deal cost? Well, it costs this many pages, like the bill I hold in my hands, in detail regulation. It is a detailed regulation that does not regulate how companies should behave, but how states, regions, municipalities should behave. It is an enormous regulation of detail. It is not free trade, it is reregulated trade. The regulatory burden is placed on our municipal employees rather than on those companies that want to act, which sometimes have to adapt to national rules designed according to national democratic procedures. If you're going to buy the contract, get so little benefits but so much disadvantages, it's like buying a car to get the steering wheel muff for free. It's not worth the price. We can certainly say that it will cost half a million euros to pay Sweden's share in the new court. I'm sure we can say that. The rest is speculation. What are we gonna do? Well, we're supposed to facilitate each other's trade by opening up the service sector. It is sad that it is not clear in the bill what is actually happening. For those who are ambitious enough to actually read the agreement, it is not always easy to see what is happening. Let us read Article 9(7). Previous articles deal with market access and market opening. Then there is the opportunity to make reservations. In Article 9(7), point (c), it is clear that those Articles do not apply to:- an amendment to an incompatible measure referred to in point (a) to the extent that the amendment does not reduce the compatibility of the measure, as it was immediately prior to the amendment, with Articles 9(3), 9(5) and 9(6), that is the stand-still and rate clauses. But that's not in the deal. You have to think of this yourself with your knowledge of how other trade agreements work and how market access works. But the small sentence of the thick agreement is the big difference compared to previous agreements. This is the first time that we have taken a different approach, also in Article 9, namely that we are opening up all service sectors, and then we can make exceptions. This is new, and we should have discussed it more clearly. All other previous agreements have deliberately opened up certain sectors. An exception can then be made in two separate annexes. We have done so in some areas. But the exceptions are not comprehensive. For example, the EU has made an exception for health that is not comprehensive. Let us look at the National Board of Trade's analysis of this issue. I really think that on the whole they have done a good analysis. The Swedish Board of Agriculture's and the National Board of Trade's assessment is that the tariff quotas for beef and pork given via CETA will not affect Swedish animal welfare or agriculture. The analysis can be criticised on two grounds: on the assumption that Swedish consumers have paid for Swedish meat at the expense of cheaper foreign meat is not tested in a recession; on the other hand, CETA is treated as an isolated agreement rather than being weighed together with future agreements, such as Mercosur and the USA, if it is done, where cheap meat under similar production forms is given tariff quotas on the EU market. There is a legitimate concern that this cheap meat could distort competition and thus lead to internal pressure on Swedish standards. We already have that pressure compared to the EU. Are we going to exacerbate that pressure through the agreement with Canada? No, I say. In addition, there is another problem here. Our agreements with Canada and the duty-free quotas are based on compliance with our minimum requirements. However, the agreement is being reached with the federal authority, but in Canada control is decentralised to the regional level. This means that we will not have much opportunity to ensure that the meat that comes is in accordance with the agreement. I would now like to address the issue of investment protection. Why have investment protection? You can really ask yourself that. The US Department of Commerce has ranked Sweden globally in terms of a safe and good climate for investment. We have nothing to gain from strengthening investment protection for Canadian companies. They already have a freedom of competition to locate their business in Sweden. If they choose Europe, it is smart to place it in Sweden. We're not making any money out of this. Besides, there's an illogical connection here. The new dispute settlement mechanism is modern and progressive. It is true that transparency has increased somewhat. It is true that there are some possibilities for third party submissions, and that is an improvement. But it's not very progressive. There are two different ways of seeing the logic of how you get compensation for indirect expropriation, i.e. that companies sue states to get money. The traditional effect is sole effect. I don't even know what it's called in Swedish; I've never read a Swedish thesis in this area. With sole effect, you look at what has happened to the company. If it's made any losses, the government will pay. There is also the more modern concept of police powers. This takes into account, as the agreement with Canada does, whether one has had a legitimate purpose to regulate something. In such cases, you can make regulations and avoid compensation if you are lucky. However, this is not to be interpreted by a Swedish court, but rather by the arbitration court. No one knows how the arbitral tribunal is to interpret this. If there is uncertainty that even the National Board of Trade acknowledges in its report. So no one knows today whether or can safely say that we will not be sued or lost. What we know for sure from previous trade agreements is that it has a regulatory cooling effect. I see that the President thinks I speak for a long time. I asked for 40 minutes' speaking time, but it was said that the tradition is 12, so I warned the President that it might be up to 40 minutes anyway. Let's see if I can be a little faster. Let me get back to the subject. There is therefore a possibility that companies may lose. But what we have seen is that the purpose of lawsuits is increasingly to achieve what we have seen from previous trade agreements and experiences from there: we want to bring about a regulatory cooling effect. It means that countries would like to do things but do not dare for fear of being sued. We can see this in the example of Philip Morris, who sued Australia when it wanted neutral cigarette packets without brands and advertising. The company probably knew from the beginning that it would lose, but the purpose was not to win. The aim was to frighten New Zealand, Ireland and England, who wanted to introduce similar legislation. However, this legislation was delayed due to the ongoing legal process. It does not help that texts say that there is a reduced risk of losing legal processes. The aim has increasingly been to scare states and have a regulatory cooling effect. There is research that shows what the effects would be if the purpose is no longer to win but to frighten states. This may, among other things, lead companies to gain a lower share of the proceedings, to make their claims more public, and to a lower degree of conciliation. All three of these indicators would suggest that companies are incoherent with us countries. This is exactly what we have seen. The number of settlements is falling, the number of profits for companies is falling and the number of high damages published is increasing. The purpose is scare tactics. Why should we open up to scare tactics when they have adequate Swedish and Canadian courts to go to? I would also like to say something about the environment and public health and the protection of investment. It is no coincidence that the chapters dealing with occupational safety, public health and the environment are excluded from the dispute settlement mechanisms. This means that if states want to sue states – that possibility also exists – there is an exception for these three areas. If the aim of the agreement had been to achieve the objectives of high environmental protection, high health protection and high levels of protection of workers, these areas would be covered by the possibility of being sued. There would have been a compliance mechanism to protect the environment and public health, but it does not. This shows that the focus of the agreement is to promote the interests of companies at the expense of States. It's no wonder. Along with some others, I have written a motion on the matter. In the motion, we called for this to be further investigated because the agreement contains such new mechanisms. This applies, for example, to the negative list of market access as well as the ratchet clause and the stand-by clause, which prevents us from re-regulating activities that we want to re-regulate without having to pay damages. Regulative cooperation, which has previously been mentioned in this House, is also a problem. The aim of regulatory cooperation is to promote trade and remove barriers to trade. We say here that we have equivalent standards to Canada and that we should trust each other's laws and rules. When the agreement was signed with the United States, it was also applicable to Canada. However, it is in the United States that the car industry exists. The example of crash tests was then taken. It seemed unnecessary to crash testing in both the US and the EU. However, a university investigated what would happen if we applied American crash tests in Europe and in the United States. It turned out then that because of road construction, speed limits, how to design roundabouts and so on, you crash in different ways. Not surprisingly, the American crash tests were better at predicting collisions in the US and the European ones were better at collisions in the EU. If we were to trust each other's tests, several hundred more people would die in traffic every year and thousands would be injured. Thus, even the example of the parade we had for us to cooperate with the rules proved to be a very bad example. To see what these regulatory simplifications have already led to, we can look at the Fuel Quality Directive. There we have already seen effects as the US and Canada threatened us if we wanted to move on with it. The same applies to legislation on cloning. There the threat of the agreements has already prevented Europe from moving forward with the legislation that we would have liked. Another good example is the mining industry. Sweden has very favourable mining rules, but it does not protect us from threats if we do anything about these rules. Even if we were just trying to adapt to other countries' standards, there is a risk that the value of investment will be weakened or completely eliminated. For example, if we introduce a substantial mining tax, which may be necessary to preserve resources for future generations, or preserve the water of Vättern or other interests in society, such as the right to land of the Sami people, there is a constant risk of lawsuits. We need to design it exactly according to the regulatory framework in order to make legitimate demands for the protection of public health and the environment. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-1169	Madam President, I would like The basic question is: What is the benefit and purpose of CETA? What problems is the agreement supposed to solve? Who benefits from it, and who pays? Carl Schlyter – and many here, to be said – has responded in a commendable way to much of all of this. And why this haste to take decisions when we do not even know whether the dispute settlement mechanisms of the agreement are legal under EU law? We MPs are elected by the people. We represent the Swedish people, not transnational, large companies. As elected representatives, we have a responsibility to think for ourselves and to take on board new information, even when it comes a little late, like the judgment of the European Court of Justice that has been mentioned here, about the illegality of the dispute settlement mechanism in the agreement between Slovakia and the Netherlands. We also have a responsibility to distinguish between large and small issues, decisions that are easy to change if circumstances change and other decisions that are impossible or at least very difficult to change. CETA is a major, far-reaching issue with huge consequences in many areas that have been well highlighted in the debate here today. Once the agreement is concluded, it is said to be very difficult to get out of it or, for that matter, renegotiate it. We have an obligation as Members of Parliament to carry out a power analysis on issues of this magnitude, and we shall also require adequate basis for decision-making. This, of course, includes thorough impact assessments of what such a complex agreement might mean, and where it actually fails. The National Board of Trade has done its job. They had a short time, but they also asked, for example, the Swedish Chemicals Inspectorate and the Swedish Environmental Protection Agency about how they viewed the agreement and what consequences it could have. They did not have time to do the really thorough analyses, and I think they should have done so. The same applies to the new information that has now come from the European Court of Justice, which stated a few weeks ago that the arbitration clause in the investment agreement between the Netherlands and Slovakia is contrary to EU law because these courts operate completely outside the national and European legal systems, thereby limiting the powers of national and European courts. The Court of Justice also found that the transfer of disputes from the European legal order to these arbitration tribunals itself constitutes a threat to the European legal order. We have all received this information from Christina Ecke, professor of EU law at the University of Amsterdam, and Marja-Marja-Liisa Öberg at Örebro University. They are experts in this area, and they advise us MPs to await the opinion of the European Court of Justice before deciding on ratification of CETA. The statement we are to wait for is therefore what Belgium has asked the European Court of Justice, i.e. whether the similar arbitration procedure in CETA is contrary to EU law or not. This may take some time, but it is only good, because then we will actually have time to get to the impact assessments. We have time for a lot of things that might be important. There's no cow on the ice either. It is not that it is not traded and invested between Canada and the EU, because it is. This is kind of funny, really. Those who advocate that Sweden ratify CETA and that it is very urgent come with dual messages. On the other hand, it is extremely important – really important – to get this in place quickly, and there is almost no difference from what it is like now. What's it gonna be? Why, for example, should we have special courts that give companies the right to sue states outside national legal systems when there are already national legal systems that work? If it is true that CETA's mechanism does not add anything new that the Trade Minister said – it was a pity that she left, because it would have been interesting to hear her develop this a little more – what should we have these new mechanisms for? I really don't understand. They say they shouldn't be so dangerous, but put it down. Don't introduce them. I don't understand. It is also said that the agreement would increase trade and that it would bring benefits in terms of cheaper goods and more jobs, but even those who are very optimistic believe that the effects would be small. One might also wonder, as several have been here: At what price? Why is trade in itself, ever-increasing trade in long-distance trans-Atlantic transport in this case and with equivalent goods that we have also heard here – half of the world's trade in equivalent goods – more important than the environment, health, trade union rights, animal welfare and even democracy? It's really weird. Trade is a means of making things better. Trade is not an end in itself. Aren't we agreed on that? It is only in trade and trade barriers that there are sanctions, sharp wordings, special courts and the ability to sue for companies, but not for states, as many have said here, but there are no similar sanctions to protect the environment, climate, trade union rights and other important values in functioning societies. This is, as Carl Schlyter said, out of fashion. It's not progressive. It's a misthinking. Trade is a means, not a goal. There are, as I said, great costs in the short and long term of such an agreement. We have already had a premonition as to how it might be in terms of perhaps not directly paid values, but it is clear that there will be a cost in the long term, in the form of the EU Fuel Quality Directive adopted in 2014. It was aimed at reducing emissions from fuels through, among other things, the labelling of more climate-damaging fuels but was voted down in the European Parliament after pressure from Canada to put CETA at risk. This made it easier to export the highly climate-damaging tar sands to the EU. Much is, as I said, very unclear and interpretable in CETA, for example with legitimate reasons to legislate to protect the environment and health. What's legitimate? Who decides that? It is not self-evident in any way. Here, of course, we have the explanation for the function and purpose of investor protection. Only the possibility for companies to sue states if they legislate to protect values other than trade will lie down as a wet blanket over legislation and over the will to legislate. It is clear that you censor yourself when you know that there can be enormous claims for damages. It has been in the debate but I would like to reiterate that Canada is a major mining nation and that Sweden has legislation that is extremely favourable to mining companies if they want to operate in our country. Romania was sued for a sum equivalent to half the country's health care budget by a Canadian mining company because their mining project was stopped for environmental reasons. The most important thing is not whether companies win or not. Only the threat of lawsuits and the risk of being forced to pay enormous damages go a long way. What would happen if the Swedish parliament wanted to tighten up the requirements for running mines and taking greater account of the environment, drinking water, Vättern, the North Kärr mine, the locals, the reindeer industry or if you would raise the negligible, ridiculously low, mineral fees? What would happen? There's a lot at stake. We'd take big risks. For what? For big companies to make more money. It is not our job as Members of Parliament. Sweden should join France's line. They advocate a climate veto in CETA, and they investigate and wait to ratify CETA. It is wise, and there we could hang on and get stronger along with a progressive force in this case. I'll ask you again, what's the deal? Who's adding anything to that? And who's paying? You should all ask yourselves those questions before you vote yes. We have a responsibility as individuals and as elected members of this Assembly. CETA is to a very small extent about free trade. There is not much toll between the EU and Canada, and removing those that exist is great but does not require this comprehensive agreement, because it is about regulatory cooperation and investment protection. The effect of entering into this agreement is that even more power is shifted to already powerful big companies and away from the arenas of democracy. Why should the parliament, which represents the people, decide to just give away so much power to already powerful interests that are not democratically governed and that actually exist to generate profits to owners, usually shareholders? Furthermore, if we await the ruling of the Court of Justice of the European Union on the agreement, even in all its parts, is legal under EU law, we will have time for the broad democratic process that has not yet taken place. I have already said that authorities such as the Swedish Chemicals Inspectorate and the Swedish Environmental Protection Agency would have time to carry out detailed impact assessments. Not least, civil society, people in general, could be involved in getting a chance to understand and take a stand on what concerns and affects us all in so many ways. How can one be concerned about the decline in confidence in democracy and the emergence of populist parties and authoritarian regimes while voting through a system-changing decision like CETA without serious public debate, without seriously inviting the public and civil society to participate? We have a chance. It can be done. It's not weird. It is therefore not only unnecessary but irresponsible, I would say, to instigate a decision before we know what the European Court of Justice will find out about the ICS dispute settlement mechanism. If the Court states that ICS is contrary to EU law, a difficult legal situation arises, if Sweden has said yes. As I said, it can be avoided. Let us wait to make decisions! We can and should wait for the verdict – and use the waiting time well, I think. In view of all this, I cannot see voting in favour of CETA today as anything other than a kind of democratic self-harm, and I do not think we should engage in that. I would like to conclude by calling for the approval of Amendment No 1, which proposes rejecting the proposal, and also for the request to refer the matter back to committee.

Let us remember what it was seven years ago in this House. When SD was a new party, we suggested that the training of conscripts be resumed. Then we were mocked for this. The employee defense was available and useful, it was said. It is true – the Swedish units are of high quality. An employed unit is better than a military unit on most crucial points. The problem with employee units is that they are expensive and therefore can not be that many. Today's only brigade, with the aim of making the brigades two, is inadequate. If we were attacked, we would have to go through difficult and extensive transport. If transport to the battle zone were to succeed, the risk is that we would have lost the favored role of defending units and would rather have the role of the attacker regaining ground where an attacker secured terrain. We must have more brigades so that we are peace-grouped in the areas where an attacker can try to establish himself. The SD has proposed eight army brigades and three amphibian brigades, consisting of just over two-thirds conscripts. To increase the number of trained conscripts, we propose, first of all, that 8 600 be trained annually. We propose the appointment of all young men and voluntary appointments for young women. Everyone should go through a three-day course in uniform in connection with the signing of a contract in order to create participation. According to the urgent wishes of Norrbotten, a recruitment office should be opened in Norrland to stimulate recruitment in the northern parts of Sweden. More women should be attracted to defence through campaigns, not through quotas. The brigade sketch we use is the one that Stellan Bojerud brought out for us when he was in our parliamentary group. In it, the first battalion, Brigade Staff, Brigadier Air Guard and Brigadier Patrol are fully employed. This makes every brigade in our system directly accessible and easier to mobilize. In its perspective study, the Swedish Armed Forces sketches a Swedish defence in 2035 with five army brigades and six amphibious battalions. Madam President, I would like Now the Armed Forces have told us what you need. Now it has to be estimated and the defence budget increased accordingly. The government must stop slowing down the armament of the defense. The issue of defence can no longer be stuttered. The training of conscripts has at least two additional positive effects. Those who have done military service constitute a staff pool from which more people can choose to take up employment in the defence. The compulsory service training therefore strongly favours the recruitment of contracted soldiers. Another reason why the training of conscripts is positive is the popular attachment of the defence. With only one defence employed, the employed soldiers and officers will easily be the only ones to solve the total defense task. However, a sustained defence requires that most of the people and preferably the whole people feel involved. As a result of compulsory education, those trained and their relatives and friends begin to feel involved. Duty is good. In the Swedish Armed Forces, approximately 20 000 people work daily. The staff of the Swedish Armed Forces are now divided into continuous staff employed by the Swedish Armed Forces, and at times employed by the Swedish Armed Forces, who have another employer in essence. In addition to employed personnel, there are also women and men who have signed service agreements with the Swedish Armed Forces. This takes place mainly within the Home Guard. The continuous staff consists of around 9 000 professional officers, 5 300 soldiers and seamen, and about 5 400 civil workers. Our system of periodically acting staff and staff with service agreements – i.e. people who do not have the Swedish Armed Forces as their main employer – is unique to us in Sweden. The temporary staff consists of approximately 6,500 reserve officers and 4,300 soldiers and sailors. The Home Guard was estimated at the turn of the year to be 21,700 women and men. In total, we have 50 000 people in the Swedish Armed Forces. Sweden can therefore put 50 000 soldiers on its feet. We can compare ourselves with our Nordic neighbours, which may not be entirely fair because we have some different systems. Norway has 45 000 people in its defence, and Denmark has 63 000 people, but it should also be remembered that they are NATO countries which, in the event of abuse, can be helped by other NATO countries. Finland, like ourselves, is not part of NATO. Finland can stand with 253 000 people in its defence. The fact that Sweden has a low number of people in the Swedish Armed Forces obviously means a certain vulnerability. i-a3d72c2ab376980f-1276	Mr President, I would like to We are now debating certain questions about the Armed Forces' personnel. I was going to dwell on our reservation No. 5, which is about a more equal defence. For the Left Party, the military freedom of alliance is central. It is through an independent foreign policy and an international credibility that we have the opportunity to build security together with others. A credible Swedish military alliance freedom also means a need for a Swedish defence with strong popular support, which has the capacity to maintain control over Swedish territory as well as to meet the various security threats that we face and be able to contribute in international efforts to safeguard peace and security. We therefore welcome the decision to reintroduce military service. It is excellent that both men and women are called to sign. It is an important step for a more equal and more popularly established Swedish defence. Although the new military service takes place on a relatively small scale, it is an important step in the right direction. In the long term, we want to further develop military service, and it should be possible to do both military service and civilian service. The fact that defence is made up of people from different backgrounds and experiences is ultimately a question of democracy. It is really now that military service really becomes universal because both men and women are given the opportunity to sign. The proportion of men and women who do military service should be closely monitored and evaluated so that we achieve a balanced gender balance. However, it is necessary to strengthen efforts to increase gender equality in defence. Our society consists of half women, and it must also be reflected in the Swedish Armed Forces. The military represented the last male professional monopoly in Sweden. It was only in 1989 that women were allowed to work in the armed forces. In the previous conscription only men were summoned to the conscription, and it was not until 1994 that women were allowed to do conscription without at the same time intending to carry out the training of officers. Although Sweden was the first in the world to allow women to work in all military positions, many notions of male and female still exist. Women were, and to some extent, seen as part of the nation to be protected. Nevertheless, women have for a long time been involved in defence in various ways, including through support organisations such as the lottery corps, the largest women's organisation that Sweden has ever had. Today, the Armed Forces' personnel consist of approximately 87 percent of men. Among officers and special officers, the share is 94.5 per cent, and among group officers, soldiers and sailors it is about 88 per cent. Among civilian employees, the distribution is somewhat more even, although men with their 62 per cent also represent a majority. The increase in the number of women employed is too slow. The large proportion of men represents a democratic deficit. Sweden today has a different gender distribution in the defence than many Western countries. The Swedish Armed Forces' own action plan for gender mainstreaming applies during the period 2015– 2018. However, it lacks sufficient measurable quantitative and qualitative targets. In order to achieve the requirements for participation in Resolution 1325, the Swedish Armed Forces' gender work must be intensified. The Left Party wants to see a defence force where women and men are equal and where women are represented to the same extent as men, regardless of their position. When today's newborns turn 18 and are called to sign, they must meet with an equal defence force. The Swedish Armed Forces should be instructed to come back with a plan for achieving a gender balance at the various levels of the organisation by 2035. The Swedish Armed Forces should also be charged with carrying out an annual gender equality survey. In November 2017, the Swedish Armed Forces' response to the Metoo came through the call # attentiontochbitihop, which 1,730 women who are or have been active within the Swedish Armed Forces have signed. The defence personnel testify to everything from offensive comments and bullying to rape and believe that there is a culture of silence within the Swedish Armed Forces where complaints are not taken seriously. Among other things, a female officer tells of an event when she entered the shared coffee room one afternoon, a coffee room that they also shared with civilian women. There were men sitting in all the couches, and they were drunk and they were playing a porno while they were drinking coffee. When the woman protested, she was mocked and forced to leave the room. This is just one in a series of examples of offensive and discriminatory treatment and far from the worst example. The Armed Forces are still too much an organisation of men, for men. It is essential that women are taken seriously and that a functioning system for reporting is established within the Swedish Armed Forces. It is gratifying that the ÖB has stated that there should be zero tolerance to sexual harassment within the Swedish Armed Forces. Now we have to live up to this vision in practice. A more equal representation within the Swedish Armed Forces is also important for developing a peace and security policy on a feminist basis, something that the Left Party wants to see. Security policy is not gender neutral. Women and children are the hardest hit in a conflict. Without a gender perspective, there is an imminent risk that men's experiences and experiences in security policy will be taken as a starting point. Security policy must therefore include a clear gender perspective if it is to cover the entire population and be long-term and successful. It is important that Sweden acts both nationally and internationally to improve the work with UN resolutions 1325 and 1820. In addition to increasing the proportion of women in peace-promoting efforts, there must be a gender perspective on international efforts. For example, international workers have repeatedly committed sexual abuse of women and girls. Freedom of communication must also apply to employees of the Swedish Armed Forces who point out abuses of sexual violence, whether they work in Sweden or internationally. I request the approval of reservation 5.

i-a3d72c2ab376980f-1391	Mr President, I would like to Let me first agree with Lotta Johnsson Fornarves and Alexandra Völker's opening remarks, which highlighted the importance of working with gender equality within the Swedish Armed Forces. I am also in favour of the clear position that has come from the Swedish Armed Forces on how to deal with these issues. However, I was a little more worried when I read on the Swedish Armed Forces Facebook page on 8 March, where they clearly communicated the importance of working with a broader recruitment. There were hordes of comments that marked their rejection of the Swedish Armed Forces precisely because they are trying to push in a positive direction, so there is still a lot to do. Very well! The questions about the Armed Forces' personnel, which the House is now discussing, are among the key issues in order to ensure a well-functioning armed forces in the coming years. When the Swedish Armed Forces now reset, strengthen their operational capabilities and regain abilities that we previously prioritised away, knowledgeable employees are required for this to succeed. For a number of years, the Swedish Armed Forces' staff has been under strain, which can be linked to the suspension of military service in 2010. We environmentalists were fundamentally in favour of providing the Swedish Armed Forces entirely on a voluntary basis. Unfortunately, it has been shown that we did not manage to bring together enough people who wanted to do military basic education voluntarily. In order to secure the Armed Forces' need for personnel, we have therefore supported the line of restoring the duty, but with the new focus that clearly emphasises that the selection for basic education should be based on the individual's interest, motivation and will. The investigation into the defence's personnel supply, which paved the way for the newly-introduced duty, is pressing for a series of measures to strengthen incentives to volunteer for the Swedish Armed Forces and to better reach out to young people who might be interested. It is important that the Swedish Armed Forces continue to work to attract more young people to volunteer for basic education, because it is those who are interested and motivated who are most likely to then take up employment. Not least now that the Swedish Armed Forces are expected to grow, it is necessary that the organisation is a good employer for whom it is attractive to work. Moreover, we have a responsibility to our young people not to use the instrument of duty more than necessary. On this basis, I would also like to stress my scepticism about using this duty as a training institution or an integration project. The Swedish Armed Forces will of course work to reflect the entire society. But the instrument of duty is a powerful tool and a power that the state exercises over its citizens, and it should therefore be used with care. Therefore, its use should be justified only by ensuring Sweden's safety. The fact that people from different backgrounds meet is important, but it is not the task of the Swedish Armed Forces to fulfil that role. Moreover, it is not possible for the defence to fulfil that role at a time when only a small part of Sweden's young people undergo military basic training. A return to duty for large parts of or whole years is not economically feasible, and that is not what the Swedish Armed Forces are asking for either. Since the duty is to be used with care, I am also satisfied with the wording that the Defence Committee agreed on regarding basic education with civil duty. It states that a basic education with a civil obligation should only be considered if it is clear that the needs cannot be addressed with other tools. Mr President, I would like to Swedish defence is growing, and I know that the management is actively working on staff issues. Recruiting new talent, keeping old and securing a good working environment from the account tied to the foreign service is an important challenge that we will have reason to continue to follow. I therefore call for the committee's proposal to be approved in the report.
